## **EXECUTIVE SUMMARY**

- A re-survey of coral communities in the American Samoa Archipelago covering the island of Tutuila and the Manu'a Group of islands (Ofu, Olosega, and Tau), was carried out during March 2002.

--- SENTENCE 132 ---
The principle objective was to assess the status of coral communities and to provide detailed analysis of temporal change between the initial baseline coral survey of 1995 and the present survey of 2002.

--- SENTENCE 133 ---
A second objective was to recommend areas for future consideration for inclusion into Marine National Parks.

--- SENTENCE 134 ---
- Overall, recovery from cyclone disturbances in the 1990s is progressing well on Tutuila and Tau Islands.

--- SENTENCE 135 ---
However, the islands of Ofu and Olosega in the Manu'a Group have been affected by a chronic crown of thorns starfish infestation that is severely disrupting recovery processes on reef slope communities.

--- SENTENCE 136 ---
In addition, the impact of recent bleaching events has been probably most pronounced in shallow water, with relatively lower incidence of bleaching 10 m depth in the majority of sites, particularly in the Manu'a Group.
- All surveyed sites in 2002 were restricted to the 10 m deep slope habitat only.

--- SENTENCE 137 ---
The 1995 baseline survey utilised a larger sample size (belt width) within replicate transects, that could not be duplicated in the allocated time for the present survey due to the significant increase in colony densities.

--- SENTENCE 138 ---
The same number of belt transects (5 per site) was used in both surveys for the Manu'a Group sites, but the number of transects per site was reduced to 3 per site on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 139 ---
Consequently, sample unit size per site was 50% to 70% of the area sampled in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 140 ---
- Data were analysed to investigate changes in coral colony size structure, cover, and density, and to interpret community dynamics.

--- SENTENCE 141 ---
The variation in sample effort between surveys and to a less extent, among sites in 2002, meant that pooled analyses were limited, so descriptive and statistical tools were used to interpret community dynamics during the period between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 142 ---
Threats and disturbances were also interpreted from features of the coral community dynamics, which included the impacts (and current extent) of coral bleaching as well as crown of thorns starfish infestations.
- Despite the reduction in sample effort in 2002, approximately similar numbers of colonies were recorded in both surveys (10,028 colonies in 2002 compared to 12,640 colonies in 1995, resulting in higher colony density estimates at the majority of sites in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 143 ---
- Overall, a marked increase in species richness was observed in more than half the sites from the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 144 ---
Changes in species richness varied among sites on Tutuila between 2002 and 1995.

--- SENTENCE 145 ---
Three of the four Pago Harbour sites recorded a reduction in species richness but this may be due to the reduction in sample effort.

--- SENTENCE 146 ---
In contrast, six of the eight sites in the Manu'a Group recorded higher species richness

- in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 147 ---
Overall, there was a moderate to high similarity in the most dominant species and genera in both surveys
- Six of the twelve sites on Tutuila recorded similar structural (growth form) dominance in both surveys, with the remaining sites equally split between those with moderate and low structural similarity.

--- SENTENCE 148 ---
Five out of eight Manu'a Group sites recorded similar structural dominance between both surveys.

--- SENTENCE 149 ---
- A half of the sites on both Tutuila and the Manu'a Group recorded high species turnover.

--- SENTENCE 150 ---
Two of the twelve sites on Tutuila, and three of the eight sites in the Manu'a Group, recorded both high structural similarity and high species turnover.

--- SENTENCE 151 ---
- The patterns in species richness, growth form dominance, and species turnover, indicate that Tutuila Island sites are far more variable in coral community dynamics than are the Manu'a Group of sites.

--- SENTENCE 152 ---
- Crown of thorns starfish (COTS) impacts were particularly evident in the Manu'a Group on the islands of Ofu and Olosega.

--- SENTENCE 153 ---
The relatively recent presence of COTS on these islands were interpreted from the contrasting pattern in colony size, frequency of coral fragmentation, the nature of coral fragments, and reduction in coral cover, in comparison to most other sites on Manu'a and Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 154 ---
Evidence of the presence of above normal densities of starfish on these islands indicates that the full extent of their impact may not be complete at present.
- Patterns of bleaching were highly variable on the slopes at 10 m depth with higher rates of bleaching observed in the Manu'a Group (11.8% of colonies were bleached) than on Tutuila (2.3% of colonies bleached).

--- SENTENCE 155 ---
An average of 8% of all colonies from the total survey data was bleached.

--- SENTENCE 156 ---
Massive corals were most frequently bleached at 10m depth (eg, *Montastraea curta*, *Goniastrea retiformis*, and *Porites* cf *lutea*).

--- SENTENCE 157 ---
Corals from five of eleven sites on Tutuila did not show signs of bleaching at 10 m depth though the situation was different in shallower water.

--- SENTENCE 158 ---
The sites that were free of bleaching were generally the most turbid sites.

--- SENTENCE 159 ---
Most bleaching symptoms at 10 m depth was minor with usually a pale colour being evident.

--- SENTENCE 160 ---
- Pago Harbour sites are of particular interest to DMWR and other government agencies due to health concerns and attempts to improve water quality.

--- SENTENCE 161 ---
The results of the latest surveys are encouraging but equivocal.

--- SENTENCE 162 ---
Colony density and coral cover increased in all four sites by 2002 and similar cover was recorded in the other two sites in both surveys.

--- SENTENCE 163 ---
Overall, colony sizes were more evenly spread and there was an increase in the number of larger colonies between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 164 ---
Two sites (Onesosopo and Aua) showed moderate and high structural similarities, respectively, with a similar dominance (low species turnover) between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 165 ---
One site (Fatumafuti) had a noticeable reduction in species richness as well as a reduction in colony sizes, resulting in low structural similarity and high species turnover between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 166 ---
Species richness in the Pago Harbour sites are within the range recorded from elsewhere on Tutuila, though only one of the four sites showed an increase in richness

in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 167 ---
Coral cover was generally lower in Pago Harbour than at other Tutuila sites, except for Fagafue and Fagasa.

--- SENTENCE 168 ---
It was concluded that two of the Pago Harbour sites (Faga'alu and Fatumafuti), which are both on the outer SW side of the Harbour, could have been (or are still) affected by unknown disturbances during the period between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 169 ---
Recommendations for Marine National Park considerations include the slope communities of Aunu'u Island near Tutuila (due to the diversity of corals), and the large bay opposite this site called Fagaitua.

--- SENTENCE 170 ---
Fagaitua has an unusual species assemblage with respect to other sites on Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 171 ---
Afuli, on the island of Tau, is also worthy of consideration due to the large sizes of *Porites* spp and *Diploastrea heliopora* colonies.

--- SENTENCE 172 ---
The northern side of Tau is also noteworthy due to the relatively diverse coral community and the low threat of impact from land use practices on the adjacent landmass.

--- SENTENCE 173 ---
## **Coral Community Features from the 2002 survey (All photos by Larry Basch).

--- SENTENCE 174 ---
The crown of thorns starfish (COTS) *

--- SENTENCE 175 ---
Acanthaster planci*, has had a significant impact on slope coral communities at Ofu and Olosega Islands (Manu'a Group) between the 1995 and 2002 surveys.

--- SENTENCE 176 ---
COTS have apparently not been active elsewhere among the volcanic island reefs during the same time period.

!

--- SENTENCE 177 ---
[](_page_10_Picture_3.jpeg)

Bleached *Acropora* spp corals in the shallow lagoon at Ofu (Manu'a).

--- SENTENCE 178 ---
Widespread bleaching was observed at most sites in March 2002.

--- SENTENCE 179 ---
The degree of bleaching was greater in shallow water compared to the survey depth of 10 m.

!

--- SENTENCE 180 ---
[](_page_11_Picture_0.jpeg)

Characteristic low profile coral community at the Fagamalo site on Tau Island (Manu'a).

--- SENTENCE 181 ---
The coral assemblage is diverse and coral cover is high at this exposed slope habitat.

!

--- SENTENCE 182 ---
[](_page_11_Picture_2.jpeg)

Low profile and encrusting growth forms dominated coral communities on exposed slopes such as Fagamalo (Tau).

--- SENTENCE 183 ---
Examples are shown here of bleached colonies of *Acropora crateriformis* (centre), and of *Goniastrea retiformis* (right) and *Favia matthai* (right, below).

!

--- SENTENCE 184 ---
[](_page_12_Picture_0.jpeg)

Abundant and very large massive *Porites*

--- SENTENCE 185 ---
spp colonies as well as the unusual coral community at Afuli (Tau), makes this site worthy of consideration for inclusion in future National Park considerations.

!

--- SENTENCE 186 ---
*Astreopora* spp (middle left),encrusting *Montipora* spp (lower left), and occasional *Pocillopora verrucosa* (top left) are typical of the SE slope communities at Tau Island (along with the large massive *Porites* spp, top photo).

!

--- SENTENCE 187 ---
[](_page_13_Picture_0.jpeg)

Example of Aunu'u Island (near Tutuila Island) community where *Favia matthai* (left of clam, *Tridacna maxima*), encrusting *Montipora* spp (top left), *Pocillopora eydouxi* (above left of clam), *Pocillopora verrucosa* (above right of clam), *Stylophora pistillata* (bottom right), and coralline algae (bottom left), are vying for space.

!

--- SENTENCE 188 ---
*Acropora abrotanoides* (top) and *Acropora valida* (below) at Aunu'u Island.

--- SENTENCE 189 ---
The diverse and complex community here is recommended for inclusion in any future National Park.

!

--- SENTENCE 190 ---
[](_page_14_Picture_0.jpeg)

An unusual and flourishing coral assemblage at Fagaitua (Tutuila) that is recommended for inclusion in future National Park considerations.

--- SENTENCE 191 ---
Typical species are shown : *Acropora austera* (bottom left), *Lobophyllia hemprichii* (bottom middle), and pillow shaped *Acropora valida* scattered among the community in the middle of the photo.

!

The Fagasa (Tutuila) coral community is dominated by *Porites* species, including *Porites cylindrica* (middle bottom), and *Porites rus* (bottom right).

!

--- SENTENCE 193 ---
[](_page_15_Picture_0.jpeg)

Typical Fagasa (Tutuila) turbid water coral assemblage comprising *Porites rus* (foreground) and small to moderate size massive *Porites* spp in background.

!

--- SENTENCE 194 ---
[](_page_15_Picture_2.jpeg)

*Porites cylindrica* (middle) and encrusting *Montipora* spp (bottom left) compete for space with abundant macro algae at Fagasa (Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 195 ---
Prolific macro algae growth is a feature of this site due to favorable conditions of high turbidity and possible nutrients from terrestrial runoff, along with other factors.

!

--- SENTENCE 196 ---

Slope community at Fatumafuti (Tutuila) at the entrance to Pago Harbour.

--- SENTENCE 197 ---
Large stands of coral colonies are typical of this site.

--- SENTENCE 198 ---
Photo shows encrusting *Montipora* spp (top) and *Acropora valida* (centre, bottom).

!

--- SENTENCE 199 ---
Encrusting *Montipora* spp community dominate at Faga'alu (Tutuila), which is a relatively turbid site.

!

--- SENTENCE 200 ---
Well developed turbid water coral species are present on the inner sections of the bay at Faga'alu (Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 201 ---
As examples, *Oxypora lacera* (bottom), *Lobophyllia hemprichii* (middle right), are shown on the transect line.

Low coral cover on the slope at Onesosopo (Pago Harbour, Tutuila).

Encrusting *Montipora* spp dominate this community.

Free living *Fungia* spp amongst prolific sponge growth at Onesosopo (Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 205 ---
Macro invertebrates (other than coral) and filter feeding organisms are very common at inner sites in Pago Harbour, which is possibly due to high nutrient loads.

!

--- SENTENCE 206 ---
[](_page_18_Picture_2.jpeg)

Encrusting *Montipora* spp dominate the coral slope communities at Aua (Pago Harbour, Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 207 ---
## **INTRODUCTION**

The Department of Marine and Wildlife Resources conducts expert surveys of marine resources every 5 years on behalf of the American Samoa Government.

--- SENTENCE 208 ---
These specialist surveys are part of a broad monitoring plan (Cornish and Wilson, 2002) for the Territory.

--- SENTENCE 209 ---
and include quantitative assessments of coral, fish, crown of thorns starfish, and giant clams.

--- SENTENCE 210 ---
A companion report by Green (2002) reviews the current status of fish, benthic habitat, clams, and crown of thorns starfish, which was conducted at the same time as the coral surveys.

--- SENTENCE 211 ---
The coral baseline monitoring sites were first established in October – November 1995 (Mundy 1996), and were conducted at the same time using the same transects that were used for fish, invertebrate and habitat surveys (Green 1996).

--- SENTENCE 212 ---
In the initial baseline survey, Mundy (1996) concluded that the reefs throughout American Samoa were in a recovery phase following a combination of natural and anthropogenic disturbances, and that the communities were diverse and complex in structure.

--- SENTENCE 213 ---
His conclusions were made from a survey of 29 sites spread throughout Tutuila Island and the Manu'a Island group, where the size, density and cover of corals were estimated.

--- SENTENCE 214 ---
This report focuses on the first re-survey of corals at long term monitoring sites around Tutuila Island and the islands of the Manu'a Group, and are restricted to the 10m depth reef slope sites used by Mundy (1996).

--- SENTENCE 215 ---
## **METHODS**

## *Field Surveys*

Quantitative surveys of hard corals were carried out during March 2002 (see Green, 2002, for maps).

--- SENTENCE 216 ---
Delays in organising the re-survey meant that there was an approximate 6.3 year interval between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 217 ---
All of the sites from the 1995 survey were not resurveyed in 2002, nor were all habitats.

--- SENTENCE 218 ---
In contrast to Mundy's (1996) survey, no reef flat or lagoon sites were surveyed in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 219 ---
The current survey included a total of 21 sites made up from Tutuila Island (12 sites) and the Manu'a Islands (Ofu (3 sites), Olosega (2 sites), and Tau Islands (4 sites).

--- SENTENCE 220 ---
The site at Aunu'u Island off the SE corner of Tutuila Island is included in the Tutuila suite of sites as it is closely affiliated with this island.

--- SENTENCE 221 ---
At all sites, surveys were conducted on the reef slope at approximately 10 m depth and in the same general location as the previous surveys in 1995 (Mundy 1996).

--- SENTENCE 222 ---
One new site was established on Ofu Island during the current survey to augment the extensive survey work that has been carried out in the adjacent Ofu lagoon marine park (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 223 ---
At 11 sites on Tutuila, three replicate 20 m belt transects, with a width of 0.25 m, were surveyed for coral species size and density (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 224 ---
At one site on Tutuila (Fagaitua), a 5m length of one transect was incompletely surveyed due to lack of time.

--- SENTENCE 225 ---
In the Manu'a Group, six sites were re-surveyed using 5 replicate 20 m belt transects, with a belt width of 0.25 m. One site (Ofu Village) was re-surveyed using the same 0.5 m width in 5 replicate 20 m transects that were used in 1995, and two sites were surveyed on Tau

using variable belt widths and lengths due to the rough conditions (see Table 2 for details).

--- SENTENCE 226 ---
The baseline survey in 1995 used five replicate belt transects with 20 m length for each transect, but the belt width was 0.5 m, which is double the present survey width used in most sites in 2002 (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 227 ---
Summary of sites and methods employed in the present study compared to the initial baseline survey of Mundy (1996).

--- SENTENCE 228 ---
In all but one site (marked\*) the methods differed to the first 1995 baseline survey.

--- SENTENCE 229 ---
A separate table (Table 2) below details method variations for two sites (Faga and Lepula on Tau Island) where species groups were selected for monitoring (see text for explanation).

Sites are listed from the western end clockwise around each island, and the total surveyed area at each site is in brackets.

Usually, two divers equally shared the coral survey effort at each site.

--- SENTENCE 280 ---
It became apparent after we commenced the surveys, that colony densities were generally much higher than in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 281 ---
We decided to halve the belt width and where necessary, to reduce the number of belt transects (on Tutuila mainly) to ensure we would complete sufficient sites in the allocated time, and sample sufficient colonies at each site to make meaningful comparisons.

--- SENTENCE 282 ---
Coral communities at several sites were not re-surveyed in this study though they were surveyed in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 283 ---
These included : (on Tutuila)

--- SENTENCE 284 ---
Leloaloa slope, Utulei slope, Faga'alu lagoon, Fatumafuti reef flat, Nu'uuli slope, Nu'uuli reef flat, Amanave slope; (on Manu'a)

--- SENTENCE 285 ---
Ofu Village reef flat and Olosega reef flat.

--- SENTENCE 286 ---
Two sites (Faga and Lepula) on Tau Island (Manu'a Group) were not surveyed in the same way as the 1995 survey because of the very high density of colonies present, time constraints, and adverse sea conditions (Table 2).

--- SENTENCE 287 ---
Two divers shared the survey effort, but not generally in an equal manner.

--- SENTENCE 288 ---
As well, it was decided to survey some belt transects for all coral species and to survey additional belt transects for all non-*Montipora* spp colonies, as this genus was extremely abundant and frequently fragmented throughout the sites.

--- SENTENCE 289 ---
Consequently, extra effort was allocated to the non-*Montipora* spp component of the community (at the expense of the *Montipora* spp component) to ensure that adequate sample numbers were obtained for colony density and size class analyses of this group.

--- SENTENCE 290 ---
Table 2 shows the total area that was surveyed for the two sites of Faga and Lepula, and the areas surveyed for both non-*Montipora* spp and all species combined.

--- SENTENCE 291 ---
Methods used in the present survey (Fisk & Birkeland 2002) at Faga and Lepula (Tau Island, Manu'a Group) that varied from the standard replicate 20 m x 0.25 m belt transects.

--- SENTENCE 292 ---
Note that a number of transects included data that did not include the most numerous** *Montipora* **species (see Methods).

--- SENTENCE 293 ---
The Lepula site was re-visited on two separate dates using transects (#6, 7) that were re-deployed on the second occasion.

Each transect was surveyed by choosing usually 20-m sections of five 50-m transects that were deployed close to the substratum and parallel to the reef edge.

--- SENTENCE 314 ---
A coral was considered to be within the transect if its centre was within the belt width.

--- SENTENCE 315 ---
All corals within the belt were identified to species where possible, and the maximum diameter measured and recorded on the field data sheets.

--- SENTENCE 316 ---
Colony sizes (maximum diameter) were measured in situ to the nearest centimeter and data are presented in size categories.

--- SENTENCE 317 ---
The same size categories that were used by Mundy (1996) were used here.

--- SENTENCE 318 ---
These size class categories are :

```
1

--- SENTENCE 319 ---
= <=5cm, 
2 = >5 to <=10cm, 
3 = >10 to <=20cm, 
4 = >20 to <=40cm, 
5 = >40 to <=80cm, 
6 = >80 to <=160cm, and 
7 = >160cm.
```

Colonies that were showing signs of bleaching were noted in the field at the same time as other information was recorded.

--- SENTENCE 320 ---
Coral cover was estimated using the three point intercept sampling method, which produced percent cover data from 75 points per transect.

--- SENTENCE 321 ---
Five 50-m transects were used to estimate cover at each site.

--- SENTENCE 322 ---
Full details of the method and calculation of results are reported separately in Green (2002).

--- SENTENCE 323 ---
## *Data Analysis*

The change in monitoring methods between the two surveys was unavoidable and necessary to complete an adequate number of sites in the allocated time.

--- SENTENCE 324 ---
As well, the methods differed between surveys in the Manu'a Island Group and from Tutuila Island Group.

--- SENTENCE 325 ---
The variation in sampling methods limited the type of statistical comparisons that could be employed between the two surveys and between the two major subset groups from Manu'a and Tutuila.

--- SENTENCE 326 ---
Comparisons between surveys were predominantly descriptive and based on within-site analyses, though broader general trends were highlighted in the overall discussion.

--- SENTENCE 327 ---
Within-site analyses and discussion focused on colony density, species richness, species change or turnover, and community structure (that is, dominant growth form and size class structure).

--- SENTENCE 328 ---
It is recognised that analyses based on species presence/absence comparisons among sites and between the same sites over time are invalid when sampling effort varies.

--- SENTENCE 329 ---
This is because rare or low abundant species are more likely to be recorded as sampling effort increases.

--- SENTENCE 330 ---
Nonetheless, comparisons are made for each site using species richness (number of species) as a measure of diversity.

--- SENTENCE 331 ---
The significance of species richness comparisons was only highlighted when the number of species recorded at a site in 2002 exceeded or approximately equaled that recorded in 1995 (which was

surveyed with greater sampling effort).

--- SENTENCE 332 ---
When species richness was much lower in 2002 than in 1995, no conclusions were made from these results.

--- SENTENCE 333 ---
Most multivariate statistical analyses of data sets also assume that equal sample effort has been used so that presence/absence species lists represent a true comparative estimate of community composition among sites.

--- SENTENCE 334 ---
As the sampling regime varied between sites from the Manu'a group and Tutuila, as well as within sites from these two major site groups, results would have been less significant if analyses concentrated on those sites that were sampled in the same manner.

--- SENTENCE 335 ---
Species comparisons were only made for the top ten ranked species from each site and when the total number of colonies recorded in 2002 was similar to or greater than the colony numbers recorded in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 336 ---
In the majority of sites, these two conditions were met.

--- SENTENCE 337 ---
A low similarity between surveys in the top ranked species was defined when the top ten most abundant species had less than 4 species in common.

--- SENTENCE 338 ---
A moderate similarity was defined when the top ten species lists from both surveys had four to six species in common.

--- SENTENCE 339 ---
A high similarity between surveys was defined as both surveys having greater than six species in common.

--- SENTENCE 340 ---
G-statistic analyses for goodness of fit based on the pooled size frequencies from both surveys were included when the total number of sampled colonies was approximately equal in both survey times, and when other assumptions and restrictions on the use of the G-statistic were satisfied.

--- SENTENCE 341 ---
It was assumed that survey times and the placement of belt transects from each survey were random and that the resultant data were representative of that site.

--- SENTENCE 342 ---
The G-statistic is generated from the log-likelihood ratio, which approximates the chi-square distribution when expressed as twice this quantity (referred to as the Gstatistic, Zar 1984).

--- SENTENCE 343 ---
It is recommended that assumptions pertaining to the chi square be adhered to for the G-test (Zar 1984).

--- SENTENCE 344 ---
These include: no expected frequency be less than 1%, and more than 20% of all expected frequencies are greater than 5.

--- SENTENCE 345 ---
When these conditions are not present, it is recommended that the low frequency categories be eliminated from analyses or that adjacent categories with low frequencies are combined.

--- SENTENCE 346 ---
When a few categories could be pooled to satisfy the G-test assumptions this was carried out prior to analyses.

--- SENTENCE 347 ---
For species where size categories had too few observations such that pooling categories would not satisfy the above conditions, analyses were not carried out.

--- SENTENCE 348 ---
Chi-square tables were consulted after the G-statistic was calculated, with the degrees of freedom equaling the number of size categories used to calculate the Gstatistic minus 1.

--- SENTENCE 349 ---
The null hypothesis was that the size class frequencies are the same for both survey times.

--- SENTENCE 350 ---
The alternative hypothesis was that size class frequencies are not the same for both survey times.

--- SENTENCE 351 ---
Names for coral species were adopted from Wallace (1999) for *Acropora* species, and from Veron (2000) for some *Acropora* spp not included in the above reference and for all other species.

#### RESULTS

Overview of 2002 Survey Coral Data compared to 1995 Survey

The number of colonies that were surveyed in 2002 represented a very good estimate of the status of corals at all sites (Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 352 ---
Although the sample area was less in 2002 (generally 50-70% less area than in 1995), the total number of colonies recorded per site was not dissimilar to the total recorded in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 353 ---
A total of 10,988 colonies were recorded in transects in 2002 compared to 1995 where 12,919 colonies were recorded.

--- SENTENCE 354 ---
The density of coral colonies was consistently higher in 2002 at all sites except at Fagaitua on Tutuila Island.

--- SENTENCE 355 ---
Mean colony densities of pooled sites from the two island groups were higher in 2002 compared to 1995, and the overall mean density for all sites combined was higher.

--- SENTENCE 356 ---
The range of colony densities per site was generally higher for Tutuila in 2002 (11 / m<sup>2</sup> to  $47 / \text{m}^2$ ) compared to the 1995 range of  $4 / \text{m}^2$  to  $27 / \text{m}^2$ ).

--- SENTENCE 357 ---
In comparison, the range of densities for the Manu'a Group combined for 2002 was 9 / m<sup>2</sup> to 40 / m<sup>2</sup>, compared to 1995 (5 / m<sup>2</sup> to 21 / m<sup>2</sup>).

--- SENTENCE 358 ---
The overall mean colony density for all sites combined from Tutuila sites was higher in 2002 ( $27 / m^2$ ) compared to 1995 ( $13 / m^2$ ).

--- SENTENCE 359 ---
A very similar pattern of increase in density was recorded from the Manu'a Group.

--- SENTENCE 360 ---
The overall mean colony density combined from Manu'a sites was higher in 2002 (26.8 / m<sup>2</sup>) compared to  $1995 (10.9 / \text{m}^2)$ .

--- SENTENCE 361 ---
Overview of Coral Genera and Species Composition compared to 1995 Survey

The total number of colonies recorded in both surveys was very similar despite less sampling effort in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 362 ---
The similarity of number of colonies surveyed from both times allows some scope for broad comparisons of genus and species differences between the surveys.

--- SENTENCE 363 ---
Three genera recorded in 2002 were not recorded in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 364 ---
These genera were: *Plesiastrea*, *Lithophyllon*, and *Euphyllia* (Table 4).

--- SENTENCE 365 ---
Conversely, five genera recorded in 1995 were not recorded in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 366 ---
These were: *Coeloseris*, *Gardineroseris*, *Halomitra*, *Sandalolitha*, and *Scapophyllia*.

--- SENTENCE 367 ---
Little significance can be made of the absence of genera in 2002 as the presence or absence of genera are from taxa that represent less than 1% of the total number of colonies for any of the genera under consideration.

--- SENTENCE 368 ---
Also, it is feasible that the lower sampling effort in 2002 may account for these differences.

--- SENTENCE 369 ---
Notwithstanding the above limitation on survey comparisons of taxon differences, the relative abundance of the most abundant genera pooled from all sites was similar in both surveys (Table 4).

--- SENTENCE 370 ---
In genera representing greater than 5% of all colonies from either survey, it appears that by 2002, *Acropora* and *Goniastrea* increased in overall relative abundance, while *Pocillopora*, and possibly *Galaxea*, decreased in relative abundance.

--- SENTENCE 371 ---
The increase in overall relative abundance of *Montipora* in 2002 is probably a result of increased fragmentation in colonies from the Manu'a group where COTS have been recently active, in addition to new recruitment to the majority of sites throughout American Samoa.

--- SENTENCE 372 ---
Numbers of colonies per site that were recorded in both 1995 and 2002.

--- SENTENCE 373 ---
The density of colonies (total number of colonies / total sample area) are given in brackets.

--- SENTENCE 374 ---
Note that the total sample area differed between the two surveys at all but one site (see Tables 1,2).

--- SENTENCE 418 ---
The coral genera *Montipora* and *Porites*, clearly dominate the coral communities of the long term monitoring sites, which is similar to the situation in 1995 (Table 4).

--- SENTENCE 419 ---
As was the case from the baseline survey in 1995, two-thirds of all genera each represented less than 1% of the total number of coral colonies.

--- SENTENCE 420 ---
This indicates a continuing trend towards

increased dominance by a few genera with very little change in the relative abundance of most genera.

Summary of genera colony numbers recorded in the 2002 and 1995 surveys at comparable sites and habitats that were surveyed at both times.

--- SENTENCE 422 ---
Each genus is expressed as the percentage of the total number of colonies and is ranked with respect to total relative abundance for each survey year.

Genera with a superscript of <sup>T</sup> (Tutuila) or <sup>M</sup> (Manu'a) have only been recorded in one of these two 'island groups' indicated by the superscript in that survey time.

--- SENTENCE 437 ---
|

A total of 164 coral species and 40 genera were recorded in 2002 compared to 150 species and 42 genera from 1995 (from re-calculated species abundance lists only from sites and habitats surveyed in 1995 and 2002; Mundy 1996; Table 5).

--- SENTENCE 438 ---
The increase in species richness in 2002 is indicative of an overall continuation of recovery of coral communities throughout the archipelago.

--- SENTENCE 439 ---
Six genera from the 2002 survey were recorded to be present only on Tutuila and not in the Manu'a Group, and this was the same pattern as in 1995 for four of the six genera.

--- SENTENCE 440 ---
The genera were *Leptoseris* spp, *Alveopora* spp, *Mycedium* spp, *Caulastrea* spp, *Euphyllia* spp, and *Lithophyllon* spp.

--- SENTENCE 441 ---
The latter two genera were in very low abundance in 2002 and were not recorded in the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 442 ---
Two genera (*Turbinaria* spp and *Plesiastrea* spp) were similarly recorded in 2002 as not being present in the survey sites around Tutuila but were present in the Manu'a Group.

--- SENTENCE 443 ---
*Plesiastrea* spp was not recorded in the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 444 ---
*Oulophyllia* spp was only recorded from Manu'a in 1995 but it was recorded on Tutuila as well in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 445 ---
These patterns in generic separation are interesting but not conclusive as the entire range of habitats outside the 10 m depth slope habitat were not surveyed in detail in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 446 ---
Comparable species lists between 2002 and 1995 also have to be treated with caution due to the difference in sample effort and the rapid attenuation of the relative abundance of species when they are ranked (Tables 5, 6).

--- SENTENCE 447 ---
However, it is important to note the higher species richness in 2002 compared to 1995 despite the lower sampling effort.

--- SENTENCE 448 ---
In 2002, there were 51 species (using only confirmed identifications) that were not recorded in 1995, compared to 23 species that were not recorded in 2002 but were recorded in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 449 ---
The latter 1995 species comparison may be due to less sampling effort as most of these species were also of low occurrence in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 450 ---
Comparisons of the presence or absence of individual species from either Tutuila or the Manu'a Group is noted in the following two tables (Tables 5, 6).

--- SENTENCE 451 ---
Certain species that were only recorded from one of the two 'groups' in 1995, but were subsequently recorded in both 'groups' in 2002 include : *Acanthastrea echinata*, *Acropora verweyi*, *Cyphastrea serailia*, *Diploastrea heliopora*, *Echinophyllia aspera*, *Favia laxa*, *Favia speciosa*, *Favites abdita*, *Favites flexuosa*, *Fungia horrida*, *F.scutaria*, *Galaxea astreata*, *Goniastrea retiformis*, *Montipora millepora, Porites cylindrica*, and *Porites* sp2 (Table 5).

Summary of the presence or absence of coral species on Tutuila and the Manu'a Group from the 2002 survey.

The most relatively abundant species in both surveys did not differ substantially when data from comparable sites and habitats were compared for both surveys (Tables 5, 6).

--- SENTENCE 500 ---
A total of eleven of the twenty-three species recording greater than 1% of the total number of colonies in 2002 were also in the same abundance grouping in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 501 ---
*Montipora grisea*  was the most abundant species overall in both surveys (12.6% and 23.1% in 2002 and 1995, respectively).

--- SENTENCE 502 ---
In 2002, seven other *Montipora* species recorded greater than 1% to less than 5% of the total number of colonies, compared to 1995 where three other *Montipora* species were in the same relative abundance range.

--- SENTENCE 503 ---
Five massive species were in the same high relative abundance group (1%<Spp<5%) in 2002 compared to four massive species in the same relative abundance range in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 504 ---
Three of these massive species were common to both surveys (*Favia matthai*, *Leptoria phrygia*, *Montastraea curta*).

--- SENTENCE 505 ---
A number of encrusting species from the 2002 survey were in the 1995 relatively high abundance category used here, including : *Acropora crateriformis*, *Galaxea fascicularis*, *Leptastrea purpurea*, and *Pavona varians*.

--- SENTENCE 506 ---
Other common growth forms were the submassive *Porites rus*, and the branching *Pocillopora eydouxi.

--- SENTENCE 507 ---
Summary of the presence or absence of coral species on Tutuila and the Manu'a Group from the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 508 ---
These data are a compilation of species recorded only in comparable sites and habitats surveyed in 2002 (ie, they are a subset of the complete data recorded in 1995, see Mundy 1996).

--- SENTENCE 509 ---
Species with an \*asterisk indicate presence in the 1995 survey data but not in the 2002 survey data.

--- SENTENCE 510 ---

#### Individual Site Results and Summary

The presentation of site data is in the following order of islands: Tutuila, Ofu-Olosega (treated as a single 'island' though a small channel separates the two islands), and Tau.

--- SENTENCE 548 ---
Within each island, the result from each site commences with the site closest to the NW corner of each island and progresses in a clockwise direction.

--- SENTENCE 549 ---
Following the individual site treatments, a summary of overall trends is given.

#### **TUTUILA ISLAND**

#### Fagafue (Tutuila)

--- SENTENCE 550 ---
A similar colony count was recorded at Fagafue in 2002 and 1995 (160 cols cf 187 in 1995, Table 3), despite the sample area in 2002 being 30% of the area sampled in 1995 (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 551 ---
Consequently, there was a higher density of corals in 2002 (11 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (4 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 552 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 28, which is less than the 40 species recorded in the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 553 ---
Table 7 shows a low degree of similarity among the top ten ranked species from the two surveys, indicating that species turnover has been high.

--- SENTENCE 554 ---
The structure of the community is similar in both surveys with a dominance of encrusting forms, particularly *Montipora* spp, along with other encrusting species.

--- SENTENCE 555 ---
This site is shallow, protected, and very turbid, particularly at 10 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 556 ---
At this depth, the survey area is principally at the base of a steep slope and adjacent to a sandy substrate.

--- SENTENCE 557 ---
The high turbidity is probably due to the position of the site that is opposite a creek entrance in the small bay.

--- SENTENCE 558 ---
There is no similarity between the surveys in the rank abundance of coral species.

--- SENTENCE 559 ---
However, at genus level there is reasonable similarity.

--- SENTENCE 560 ---
In 2002, the listing of *Montipora* sp.1 refers to one of the researchers in 2002 placing specimens of (probably) *M.aequituberculata* into a separate category.

--- SENTENCE 561 ---
Though there was no similarity in the highest ranked species between the two surveys, the dominance of encrusting *Montipora* spp., submassive forms of *Porites* spp., and massive faviids, were

characteristic features of the communities at both survey times.

--- SENTENCE 562 ---
This result suggests high turnover of species is occurring at this site though ambient environmental conditions are selecting for specific growth forms.

--- SENTENCE 563 ---
Rank of top 10 most abundant species from the two surveys at Fagafue.

--- SENTENCE 564 ---
Species rank from 1995 is compared to the rank of the same species in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 565 ---
Highly ranked species from the 2002 survey which were not present in the top 10 ranked species in the 1995 survey are listed at the bottom of the 2002 survey species list.

--- SENTENCE 566 ---
FORM refers to the growth form of each species (E = encrusting; M = massive; F = foliose; S = submassive).

--- SENTENCE 575 ---
| Favites halicora           | M         | 9 |

Colony size classes differed significantly between the two surveys with a higher representation of larger colony sizes (eg, the >40 cm to <160 cm dia), and a more even spread of size classes throughout the first four size categories in 2002 (Figure 1; P<0.001, G = 21.7, df = 5).

--- SENTENCE 576 ---
In contrast, there was a clear dominance of colonies in the >10 cm to <20 cm diameter range in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 577 ---
This indicates that growth in some species groups continued over the time period between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 578 ---
Recruitment was probably occurring over the same time period as well, though there were indications that colony fragmentation was also common at this site.

!

--- SENTENCE 579 ---
Size class distribution of all species combined from the 1995 (N = 187) and 2002 (N = 160) surveys at Fagafue (Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 580 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 581 ---
Size class distribution of all** *Montipora* **species combined (top 10 ranked species) from the 1995 (N = 93) and 2002 (N = 69) surveys at Fagafue (Tutuila).

--- SENTENCE 582 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 583 ---
When *Montipora* spp were pooled for the two survey times (Figure 2), there was a very similar pattern of significant change to conclusions drawn from all pooled species

(P<0.001, G=27.9, df=4).

--- SENTENCE 584 ---
As approximately 50% of all *Montipora* spp colonies were present in the top ranked species (and approx.

--- SENTENCE 585 ---
50% of all colonies were *Montipora* spp) in both data sets, it is clear that the dynamics of this group is contributing to the main patterns of colonies at this site.

#### **SUMMARY**

There appears to have been a major shift in the relative abundance of the highest ranked species between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 586 ---
A change in the size class pattern was also evident.

--- SENTENCE 587 ---
It is concluded that this is probably a highly dynamic site and though the community structure remained essentially unchanged, the species composition did change over time.

--- SENTENCE 588 ---
High turnover may be a feature of this site due to the relatively high turbidity present (particularly at 10m depth).

--- SENTENCE 589 ---
There was evidence of a shift towards larger colony sizes in the main species groups that may have been due to growth in the dominant size cohort from 1995.

--- SENTENCE 590 ---
If this is the case, the difference in species composition may be due (in part) to differences in field identifications between the two surveys, and not from site conditions (see Taxonomic Considerations above).

--- SENTENCE 591 ---
#### Fagasa (Tutuila)

Approximately the same number of colonies was recorded at Fagasa in 2002 and 1995 (282 cols of 285 in 1995, Table 3), with sixty percent less sample area in 2002 (Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 592 ---
This resulted in a higher density of corals in 2002 (19 cols/m<sup>2</sup>) compared to 1995 (6 cols/m<sup>2</sup>, Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 612 ---

--- SENTENCE 616 ---
Size class distribution of coral colonies of all species at Fagasa from 1995 (N = 282) and 2002 (N = 285) surveys.

--- SENTENCE 617 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 618 ---
The size class distribution of all species combined at Fagasa significantly changed between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 619 ---
There was an approximately equal representation of colony sizes in the three smallest classes (<20 cm dia.)

--- SENTENCE 620 ---
in 2002, compared to a clear dominance of >10 cm - <20 cm diameter colonies in 1995 (Figure 3; P<0.001, G = 73.4, df = 4).

--- SENTENCE 621 ---
The size class distribution of the abundant *Pavona venosa* at Fagasa significantly changed between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 622 ---
There was an approximately equal representation of colony sizes in the three small to moderate size classes (>5 cm to <40 cm dia.)

--- SENTENCE 623 ---
in 2002 compared to a clear dominance of >10 cm to <20 cm diameter colonies in 1995 (Figure 4; P<0.001, G = 73.4, df = 4).

--- SENTENCE 624 ---
The size class distribution of the relatively high abundant species, *Porites rus*, also changed significantly between surveys (Figure 5; 0.01<P<0.025, G = 6.3, df = 1).

--- SENTENCE 625 ---
In 1995, this species was represented predominantly by colonies in the >10cm to <40cm diameter size range.

--- SENTENCE 626 ---
In 2002, this species was represented by a range of sizes, including a clearly dominant small colony size (<5 cm dia.).

--- SENTENCE 627 ---
This small size class represents a high recruitment pulse to this site.

!

= <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

#### **SUMMARY**

The coral community size structure has changed significantly at this site although there was little change in the dominant growth forms except for a change in *Montipora* species and greater abundance of foliose forms.

--- SENTENCE 633 ---
The density of corals has increased between surveys but there is a greater representation of smaller colonies that are due to recruitment of a few species.

--- SENTENCE 634 ---
This is a turbid site, situated in a large bay with a relatively small opening to the sea resulting in low water flushing with the open sea, and with a highly disturbed catchment.

#### Vatia (Tutuila)

--- SENTENCE 635 ---
A lower colony count was recorded at Vatia in 2002 compared to 1995 (475 cols of 1124 in 1995, Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 636 ---
Nonetheless, a higher density of corals was recorded in 2002 (32 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (22 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 637 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 58, which is less than the 53 species recorded in the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 638 ---
Table 9 shows a low degree of similarity among the top ten ranked species from the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 639 ---
The moderate to low species similarity of the most abundant species indicates high turnover of species is occurring.

--- SENTENCE 640 ---
The structure of the community is very similar between the two surveys with a dominance of encrusting forms, particularly, *Montipora* spp, but with a lower abundance of branching, massive, and submassive *Porites* species.

--- SENTENCE 667 ---
The size frequency distribution of colonies of the most relatively abundant species in both surveys, *Montipora grisea*, changed significantly with a very similar pattern to the overall combined species pattern.

--- SENTENCE 668 ---
That is, sizes changed from a dominance of >10 cm to <20cm diameter colonies in 1995 to a more even spread of size classes in 2002 (Figure 7; P<0.001, G = 50.3, df = 3).

--- SENTENCE 669 ---
Another relatively abundant species in both surveys was the encrusting *Pavona varians*, which also changed significantly in size class distribution from dominance of >10 cm to <20 cm diameter colonies in 1995, to colonies >20 cm to <40 cm diameter in 2002 (Figure 8; P<0.001, G = 17.3, df =1).

--- SENTENCE 672 ---

** 

#### SUMMARY

The coral community at Vatia continued to be diverse and dynamic over the period of the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 680 ---
Increases in colony sizes through growth, and higher colony densities through recruitment were characteristic of the site, as was the apparent turnover of species, though species richness remained high.

--- SENTENCE 681 ---
The structure of the community remained similar between 1995 and 2002 with a dominance of encrusting forms.

--- SENTENCE 703 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 36, which is less than the 46 species recorded in the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 704 ---
Table 10 shows a low degree of similarity among the top

ten ranked species from the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 705 ---
Nonetheless, the structure of the community is very similar between the two surveys with a dominance of encrusting forms, particularly of *Montipora* spp.

--- SENTENCE 706 ---
The greatest similarity between the surveys is in the very high abundance of *M.grisea*

--- SENTENCE 707 ---
but there are only three other species from the 2002 survey that are similarly ranked in the most abundant top 10 species in the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 708 ---
There are a number of highly ranked species in 2002 that are not recorded with similar high rank in the 1995 survey, and all but one species are encrusting *Montipora* species.

--- SENTENCE 709 ---
It is likely that there are some misidentifications of one or more closely related *Montipora* species between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 710 ---
For example, there are a number of *Montipora* spp that ranked high in relative abundance in the 2002 survey, but were far less relatively abundant in the 1995 survey (See list at bottom of 2002 data column, Table 10).

--- SENTENCE 711 ---
The other large difference is in the dominance of two *Porites* spp (*Porites* sp 2, and *P.* cf *horizontalata*) that have a submassive or encrusting growth form, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 712 ---
Although these species were dominant in the smallest sizes (<5 cm dia, Appendix 1), making field identification difficult, it is more likely that there has been a strong change in the relative abundance of the two species between surveys.

!

--- SENTENCE 713 ---
Size class distribution of coral colonies of all species at Masefau from 1995 (N = 1375) and 2002 (N = 676) surveys.

--- SENTENCE 714 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 715 ---
The size class distribution of all species combined show a more even spread of sizes in 2002 and more relatively abundant larger colonies (>20 cm to <80 cm dia.), compared to 1995 where there was a clear dominance of sizes up to 20 cm diameter (Figure 11).

--- SENTENCE 716 ---
This change in size classes was significant (P<0.001, G = 192.24, df = 4).

--- SENTENCE 717 ---
The distribution of size classes for the most relatively abundant species *Montipora* grisea, significantly changed from a dominance of 5 cm to 20 cm dia.

--- SENTENCE 718 ---
colonies in 1995 to a more even spread of colony sizes in 2002 (Figure 12, P < 0.001, G = 29.7, df = 3).

--- SENTENCE 719 ---
This pattern suggests that there is either continuous recruitment to the population of this species or fragmentation of colonies is occurring that is producing the smallest size classes.

--- SENTENCE 720 ---
There is also evidence of growth to larger sizes from 1995 to 2002.

#### **SUMMARY**

There has been an increase in species richness at Masefau between 1995 and 2002, and at the same time, there has been an increase in the density of colonies.

--- SENTENCE 724 ---
There has been a shift towards a more even distribution of colony sizes by 2002.

--- SENTENCE 725 ---
Also, there is high recruitment and/or fragmentation of colonies that is resulting in an abundance of small (<5 cm dia.) colonies.

--- SENTENCE 726 ---
The community structure has remained relatively unchanged since 1995 with a clear dominance of encrusting *Montipora* species.

#### Aunu'u (Tutuila)

--- SENTENCE 727 ---
A relatively high colony count was recorded at Aunu'u in 2002 (705 cols of 914 in 1995, Table 3), despite the sample area in 2002 being 30% of the area sampled in 1995 (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 728 ---
There was a higher density of corals in 2002 (47 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (18 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 729 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 56, which is marginally more than the 52 species from the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 730 ---
Table 11 shows a high degree of dissimilarity among the top ten ranked species from the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 731 ---
However, the structure of the community is very similar between the two surveys with a dominance of encrusting forms, particularly, *Montipora* spp, along with the prevalence of branching *Pocillopora* species.

--- SENTENCE 732 ---
The greatest similarity between the surveys is in the very high abundance of *M. grisea* but there are only three other species from the 2002 survey that are similarly ranked in the top 10 in the 1995 survey.

--- SENTENCE 733 ---
There are a number of highly ranked species in 2002 that are not recorded high in the 1995 survey, and all but one species are encrusting *Montipora* species.

--- SENTENCE 734 ---
The largest discrepancy between the surveys was in the dominance of *Montipora venosa* in the 2002 survey compared to it not being recorded in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 735 ---
It is likely that this is a consequence of mis-identification of one or more closely related *Montipora* species between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 736 ---
Note that there are a number of *Montipora* spp that ranked high in relative abundance in the 2002 survey, but were far less relatively abundant in the 1995 survey (See list at bottom of 2002 data column, Table 11).

--- SENTENCE 737 ---
The other large difference is in the dominance of two *Porites* spp (*Porites* sp 2, and *P.* cf *horizontalata*) that have a submassive or encrusting growth form, respectively.

--- SENTENCE 738 ---
Although the smallest sizes were dominant in these species (<5 cm dia, Appendix 1), which makes field identification difficult, it is more likely that there has been a strong change in the abundance of the two species between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 753 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 754 ---
The size class distribution of all species combined show a more even spread of sizes up to <80 cm diameter in 2002 compared to the clear dominance of sizes <10 cm diameter in 1995 (Figure 13).

--- SENTENCE 755 ---
This change in size classes was significant (P<0.001, G = 300.5, df = 4).

--- SENTENCE 756 ---
The size distribution of the most relatively abundant species *Montipora grisea*, shows a significant shift between 1995 and 2002 from small (dominated by >5 cm to <10 cm dia.)

--- SENTENCE 757 ---
to larger size classes (>10 cm to <80 cm dia., Figure 14; P<0.001, G = 125.6, df = 4).

--- SENTENCE 758 ---
A common and relatively dominant branching growth form species, *Pocillopora eydouxi*, shows a very similar size distribution pattern to *M. grisea* with a shift towards larger colony sizes (from >5 cm to <20 cm dia in 1995, to >20 cm to <80 cm dia in 2002, Figure 15).

--- SENTENCE 759 ---
This shift in size classes was significant (P<0.001, G = 32.7, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 760 ---
There also appears to have been no recent recruitment of *P. eydouxi* compared to 1995 (shown by a lack of the smallest sizes (<5 cm dia).

--- SENTENCE 761 ---
In contrast, there were only 3 juvenile *Pocillopora* spp recorded in 2002, compared to many juvenile *P. meandrina* and unidentified *Pocillopora* species in 1995.

--- SENTENCE 762 ---
*Pocillopora* spp tend to be fast colonisers of vacant substrate, and the evidence from the dynamics of this genus suggests that the community has quickly progressed to occupy most of the available space.

**SUMMARY**

Similar size class shifts to the examples given in the above figures are evident among many of the relatively dominant species at this site, indicating that the coral community has progressed to an advanced stage during the period between the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 766 ---
There has been little change in species richness at Aunu'u between 1995 and 2002 even though there has been an increase in the density of colonies.

--- SENTENCE 767 ---
There has been a shift towards a more even distribution of colony sizes from 1995 to 2002.

--- SENTENCE 768 ---
The community structure has remained relatively unchanged since 1995 with a dominance of encrusting species except for branching species becoming less relatively abundant.

--- SENTENCE 769 ---
However, the composition of the encrusting species has shifted towards *Montipora* spp (2 species in 1995 cf to 6 species in 2002 in the top ten ranked species).

--- SENTENCE 770 ---
The current coral community is a very healthy and flourishing one with space becoming limited.

effusa*, *M. grisea*, and *M. corbettensis*.

--- SENTENCE 835 ---

--- SENTENCE 837 ---

#### **SUMMARY**

The coral community at Onesosopo has continued to slowly recover from previous low levels.

--- SENTENCE 841 ---
The density of colonies and species richness has increased from 1995 with moderate turnover of species.

--- SENTENCE 842 ---
The greatest change has occurred in the number of *Montipora* species, which have increased in relative abundance between surveys.

#### Aua (Tutuila)

Relatively low colony counts were recorded in both surveys at Aua (167 cols in 2002 cf 254 cols in 1995, Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 843 ---
With the difference in sample effort taken into account, a higher density of corals was recorded in 2002 (11 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (5 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 844 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 29, which is similar to the 27 species recorded in the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 845 ---
Table 14 shows a moderate degree of similarity among the top ten ranked species from the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 846 ---
However, the structure of the community is very similar between the two surveys with predominantly encrusting growth forms present.

--- SENTENCE 847 ---
Approximately a half of the highly ranked species was represented by few colonies (less than 10 colonies) so conclusive trends would be uncertain for species other than the first 4 or 5 most relatively abundant species.

--- SENTENCE 848 ---
There was a moderate degree of similarity in the dominant species in the coral communities between the 1995 and 2002 surveys (Table 14).

--- SENTENCE 849 ---
The structure of the community was relatively unchanged with a similar dominance of encrusting forms, similar species richness, and low abundance of most species at both times.

--- SENTENCE 904 ---

--- SENTENCE 905 ---
Size class distribution of coral colonies of all species at Faga'alu from 1995 (N = 711) and 2002 (N = 376).

--- SENTENCE 906 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 907 ---
The size frequency distribution changed significantly between 1995 and 2002, with a more even spread of colony sizes in 2002 compared to the dominance of smaller size classes in 1995 (especially the >5 cm to <20 cm diameter sizes, Figure 23; P<0.001, G = 107.5, df =4).

--- SENTENCE 908 ---
The *Montipora grisea* size class distribution did not change significantly between surveys (Figure 24; 0.10<P<0.25, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 909 ---
In contrast, if the submassive *Porites* species (*P.* sp 2 in 1995 and *P.* cf *horizontalata* in 2002) were pooled, there was a significant difference in size class distributions between surveys (Figure 25; P<0.001, G = 11.7, df = 1).

--- SENTENCE 910 ---
The change in size classes for *Porites* spp (submassive) has been from a dominance of >5 cm to <10 cm diameter (along with a high relative abundance of <5 cm diameter colonies) in 1995, to a dominance of <5 cm diameter colonies in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 911 ---
This suggests that the field identifications for *Porites* spp were correct in both surveys and that there has been a change from one species to another between the surveys.

** 

#### SUMMARY

The Faga'alu coral community has shown an increase in colony density during the period between surveys, and although the species richness is similar, there has been a high turnover and apparent replacement of individual species.

--- SENTENCE 917 ---
This suggests that the site is in a recovery phase but is showing high turnover and species replacement.

The size frequency distribution of the relatively abundant *Montipora grisea* also changed significantly between surveys with a more even spread and generally larger colonies in 2002 from a dominance of smaller colonies (>5 cm to <20 cm dia.)

--- SENTENCE 991 ---
in 1995 (Figure 30; P<0.001, G = 27.4, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 992 ---
In contrast, if the submassive *Porites* species (*P.* sp 2 in 1995 and *P.* cf *horizontalata* in 2002) are pooled, there was not a significant difference in size class distributions between surveys (Figure 31; 0.25<P<0.5, G = 0.55, df = 1).

--- SENTENCE 993 ---
The size class structure for a particular species would be expected to change if the same species was present in both surveys.

--- SENTENCE 994 ---
This suggests that the field identifications were correct in both surveys and that there has been a shift from *Porites* sp 2 to *P.* cf *horizontalata* between the surveys.

#### **SUMMARY**

The coral community at Fagatele has shown a large increase in colony density and species richness between the period of the two surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1002 ---
The community has continued to recover and grow with moderate species turnover.

--- SENTENCE 1003 ---
However, the structure of the community has remained similar between surveys with predominantly encrusting *Montipora* spp and submassive *Porites* spp present.

#### Leone (Tutuila)

--- SENTENCE 1004 ---
Although the number of colonies recorded in both surveys at Leone was approximately the same (385 cols in 2002 compared to 383 cols in 1995), the density of corals was higher in 2002 (26 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (8 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 1005 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 32, which is more than the 26 species from the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 1006 ---
The higher species richness in 1995 is probably a result of the greater sampling effort in 1995 where more relatively uncommon species would be recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1007 ---
Table 18 shows the top ten ranked species from the two surveys, where a moderate degree of similarity was recorded, due to the same relative dominance of the top 3-4 species in both surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1008 ---
*Porites sp 2* was not recorded in the 2002 survey, which could mean that there was a field identification difference with possibly *Porites lichen* and/or *Porites* of *horizontalata* (both of which were only recorded in 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1009 ---
The greatest degree

of difference is in the less abundant branching *Pocillopora* spp forms and *Acropora* spp present in 2002 compared to 1995 (Table 18; Appendix 1).

** 

#### SUMMARY

The coral community at Leone appears to have undergone a reduction in abundance of branching and corymbose growth forms.

--- SENTENCE 1031 ---
This has occurred without a reduction in the most relatively dominant encrusting and submassive forms that typically have lower relief with respect to the substrate.

--- SENTENCE 1032 ---
This suggests that a physical disturbance may have occurred in the bay that was selective towards the higher relief branching forms and not the encrusting lower relief forms.

--- SENTENCE 1033 ---
In other aspects, the coral community is essentially the same as 1995 with recruitment occurring, along with a shift towards larger sizes by 2002.

#### OFU ISLAND (MANU'A GROUP)

#### Asaga (Ofu)

--- SENTENCE 1034 ---
The density of corals at Asaga was higher in 2002 (40 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (14 cols/m², Table 3), despite the fifty percent reduction in sample area (50m² in 1995, compared to 25m² in 2002, Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 1035 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 51, which is less than the 57 species from the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 1036 ---
There was a moderately similar structure and composition to the coral community at both surveys with a dominance of encrusting and massive growth forms but with species turnover occurring within these growth forms (Table 19).

--- SENTENCE 1053 ---
A higher number of *Montipora* spp were recorded in 2002, which may be a reflection of the researcher's ability to separate similar species due to relatively larger colony sizes.

--- SENTENCE 1054 ---
Alternatively, there may be a compositional change due to crown-of-thorns (*Acanthaster planci*, COTS) predation that most likely occurred at this site over the previous few years (pers.obs.).

--- SENTENCE 1055 ---
However, the structure of the coral communities was similar in both surveys with a co-dominance of encrusting and massive forms present.

--- SENTENCE 1056 ---
Additional support for this interpretation of past disturbance is the very low abundance of *Acropora* spp in 2002 (6 colonies in total) compared to 1995 (66 colonies, Appendix 1).

--- SENTENCE 1059 ---
The size frequency of all species colonies shows a significant change between the two surveys (Figure 34; P<0.001, G = 418, df = 3).

Size class distribution of coral colonies of** *Montastraea curta* **at Asaga from 1995 (N = 38) and 2002 (N = 90) surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1065 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1066 ---
The size frequency patterns for the most relatively abundant species, *Montipora grisea*, are almost identical to the combined species pattern with a significant change to smaller colonies in 2002 (Figure 35; P<0.001, G = 174.3, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 1067 ---
This was a typical pattern for the majority of species recorded in both surveys (Appendix 1).

--- SENTENCE 1068 ---
There were exceptions to the general pattern of change, for example, *Montastraea curta* did not show any significant change in colony size distributions between the two surveys (Figure 36, 0.5 < P < 0.75, G = 0.3, df = 1).

--- SENTENCE 1069 ---
Most colonies of *M. curta* exhibited signs of partial death and (frequently) fragmentation, which resulted in a similar size class distribution in both surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1070 ---
Partial death and fragmentation may have been due to either the effect of bleaching on this susceptible coral species and/or COTS partial feeding effects.

#### **SUMMARY**

Significant change has occurred at this site with a reduction in size of most colonies.

--- SENTENCE 1071 ---
The density of colonies has increased between 1995 and 2002, though there is a similar species composition present.

--- SENTENCE 1072 ---
Many species present in 2002 were fragments and were predominantly of species that can survive and re-grow from fragments (ie, encrusting and massive forms).

--- SENTENCE 1073 ---
Fragmentation is a common feature in these forms that have been subjected to COTS predation.

--- SENTENCE 1074 ---
There was a noticeable decline in the presence of *Acropora* spp, which is the preferred food of COTS, and although there were some A*cropora* spp colonies present in shallower water (<5 m depth, pers.obs.)

--- SENTENCE 1075 ---
this genus was noticeably absent from the survey depth range of 10 m depth.

--- SENTENCE 1076 ---
These conclusions support the field interpretation in 2002 that a major disturbance from COTS predation has affected the coral community at this site.

--- SENTENCE 1077 ---
#### OLOSEGA ISLAND (MANU'A GROUP)

#### Sili (Olosega)

The total number of colonies recorded in 2002 (600 cols) is less than the number from 1995 (1054 cols).

--- SENTENCE 1078 ---
This reduction in number of colonies is partially due to the smaller sample area in 2002 (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 1079 ---
Nonetheless, the density of corals at Sili (Olosega) was marginally higher in 2002 (24 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (21 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 1080 ---
The number of species recorded in 1995 was 55, which is more than the 48 species recorded in the 2002 survey.

--- SENTENCE 1081 ---
Table 20 shows the top ten ranked species from the two surveys, where a high degree of similarity was recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1082 ---
The largest difference between the surveys was in the relative abundance and rank of two *Goniastrea* spp which may be a result of differences in field identification between the surveys, though this is not likely as *G. retiformis* is a very distinctive species in the field.

--- SENTENCE 1083 ---
The other major change was in the relatively higher abundance of *Porites rus* in 2002 compared to 1995.

--- SENTENCE 1084 ---
This may be due to growth and possibly recruitment of this species during the period between surveys

or may be because of a difference in placement of the transects between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1085 ---
The latter reason is unlikely as the site is clearly defined in a position between two large bays in the reef perimeter.

#### **SUMMARY**

The coral community at Sili in 2002 was similar to the composition and density of colonies in 1995 with some noticeable exceptions.

--- SENTENCE 1121 ---
There were fewer *Acropora*

--- SENTENCE 1122 ---
spp colonies in the transects in 2002 which possibly would account for the reduction in species richness in 2002, though the sample area was half of that used in 1995 which would result in fewer species being recorded.

--- SENTENCE 1123 ---
There was also a reduction in colony size overall and among some of the most highly ranked species.

--- SENTENCE 1124 ---
Field observations are supported by the data from 2002 of the Sili site that shows there has been signs of impact from COTS feeding.

--- SENTENCE 1125 ---
These conclusions support the field interpretation in 2002 that a major disturbance from COTS predation has affected the coral community at this site.

In contrast, the dominant colony size in 2002 was <5 cm diameter (Figure 41).

--- SENTENCE 1155 ---
The size frequency patterns for the highly relatively abundant species, *Montipora grisea*, are almost identical to the combined species pattern, with a significant change to smaller colonies in 2002 (Figure 42; P<0.001, G = 52, df = 3).

--- SENTENCE 1156 ---
This was a typical pattern for the majority of species recorded in both surveys (Appendix 1).

!

--- SENTENCE 1157 ---
Size class distribution of coral colonies of** *Montipora grisea* **at Olosega Village from 1995 (N = 129) and 2002 (N = 199) surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1158 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1159 ---
The size frequency patterns for the more relatively abundant species, *Montastraea curta*, were significantly different in 2002 (Figure 43, 0.005<P<0.01, G = 9.96, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 1160 ---
The change was similar to the size structure of majority of species present in 2002, that is, a shift from larger to a dominance of smaller size colonies (<5 cm diameter).

--- SENTENCE 1161 ---
The size frequency patterns for the relatively moderate abundant species, *Goniastrea retiformis*, are almost identical to the combined species pattern and other high abundant species, with a significant change to smaller colonies in 2002 (Figure 44; P<0.001, G = 31, df = 2).

!

--- SENTENCE 1162 ---
Size class distribution of coral colonies of** *Montastraea curta* **at Olosega Village from 1995 (N = 41) and 2002 (N = 86) surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1163 ---

Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1166 ---
**SUMMARY**

Significant change has occurred at this site predominantly as a result of a reduction in size of most colonies between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1167 ---
The density of colonies has increased between 1995 and 2002, and the species composition of the most relatively abundant species has changed from encrusting and massive forms to mostly encrusting forms.

--- SENTENCE 1168 ---
Many species present in 2002 were fragments of former larger colonies and were predominantly of species that can survive and re-grow from fragments (ie, encrusting and massive forms).

--- SENTENCE 1169 ---
Fragmentation is a common feature in these forms that have been subjected to COTS predation.

--- SENTENCE 1170 ---
There was a noticeable decline in the presence of *Acropora* spp, which is the preferred food of COTS.

--- SENTENCE 1171 ---
The high number of small colonies is evidence that active coral recruitment is occurring.

--- SENTENCE 1172 ---
However, a large number of small colonies are clearly fragments as well.

--- SENTENCE 1173 ---
These conclusions support the field interpretation in 2002 that a major disturbance from COTS predation has affected the coral community at this site.

--- SENTENCE 1174 ---
## **OFU ISLAND (MANU'A GROUP)**

## **Hurricane House (Ofu)**

This is an additional site to the original baseline sites established by Mundy (1996) that was established to obtain additional data from a southern exposure habitat.

--- SENTENCE 1175 ---
It was also chosen because it is part of the Ofu Marine National Park and it was thought that the data would complement the extensive work that has been conducted in the adjacent lagoon.

--- SENTENCE 1176 ---
The density of colonies at Hurricane was 38 cols/m2 , with 73 species making up the sample.

#### **SUMMARY**

This site recorded one of the highest species richness of all sites in American Samoa in 2002, and colony density was also relatively high compared to most other sites.

--- SENTENCE 1203 ---
From field observations of fragmentation and dead standing colonies, there appeared to be evidence of impacts from COTS feeding activity that was supported by the presence of a dominance of the smallest size classes in some species.

--- SENTENCE 1204 ---
The presence of dominant encrusting and massive growth forms and the interpretation of COTS impacts on the coral community are similar to conclusions from other sites on Ofu and Olosega.

The size class distribution of all species combined changed significantly between 1995 and 2002 (Figure 48; P<0.001, G = 25.3, df = 4).

in 1995, to a dominance of smaller colonies, especially those <5 cm diameter in 2002.

--- SENTENCE 1232 ---
Size class distribution of** *Acropora crateriformis* **at Ofu Village from 1995 (N = 82) and 2002 (N = 79).

--- SENTENCE 1233 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1234 ---
Size class distribution of** *Echinopora hirsutissima* **at Ofu Village from 1995 (N = 22) and 2002 (N = 60).

--- SENTENCE 1235 ---
Maximum diameter size class categories refer to the following : 1 = <=5 cm, 2 = >5 - <=10 cm, 3 = >10 - <=20 cm, 4 = >20 - <=40 cm, 5 = >40 - <=80 cm, 6 = >80 - <=160 cm, and 7 = >160 cm.

--- SENTENCE 1236 ---
The size structure of the most abundant species *Acropora crateriformis* also changed significantly between 1995 and 2002 (Figure 49; P<0.001, G = 28, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 1237 ---
The change between surveys was from a dominance of smaller sizes in 1995 (especially <5 cm to <10 cm dia.), to larger sizes dominated by the >20 cm to <40 cm diameter class, in addition to a high dominance of recruits in the <5 cm diameter size range.

--- SENTENCE 1238 ---
This species was represented by recruits rather than smaller fragmented colonies in the 2002 small size class (<5 cm dia.), which was in contrast to many other species present (see below).

--- SENTENCE 1239 ---
One species that was abundant in both surveys but displayed a different pattern to A. crateriformis, was Echinopora hirsutissima (Figure 50).

--- SENTENCE 1240 ---
E. hirsutissima showed a significant change in size structure from an approximately normal size distribution with the most frequent class size of >10 cm to <20 cm diameter, to a dominance of small colonies (<5 cm dia.)

--- SENTENCE 1241 ---
(P<0.001, G = 15.7, df = 2).

--- SENTENCE 1242 ---
#### **SUMMARY**

The dynamics of the coral community at this site have been influenced by the continued presence of COTS in low densities from 1995 to 2002 (some individual starfish and recent feeding scars were observed in 2002).

--- SENTENCE 1243 ---
The predatory starfish are maintaining low coral colony sizes through their continued presence and feeding, which is also resulting in colony fragmentation.

--- SENTENCE 1244 ---
However, the effects from COTS are not the same for all species present and the slow increase in species richness indicates that recruitment of additional species to the community is occurring.

--- SENTENCE 1245 ---
As well, some species, eg, *A. crateriformis*, are showing high recruitment rates despite high mortality rates due to COTS predation.

--- SENTENCE 1246 ---
This species is highly desired food for COTS and because of its encrusting growth form, individual colonies can be fully eaten, leaving fewer fragments.

--- SENTENCE 1247 ---
This contrasts with other species, eg, *E. hirsutissima*, which has a foliose growth form that COTS are less likely to fully consume, resulting in many remnant fragments.

--- SENTENCE 1248 ---
Overall, the situation at this site is one where the coral community has been retained at a very early phase of recovery due to the continued presence and activity of COTS.

--- SENTENCE 1278 ---

--- SENTENCE 1279 ---

#### **SUMMARY**

The coral community at Faga has not changed substantially from 1995 with a continued dominance of massive and encrusting growth forms.

--- SENTENCE 1321 ---
Species turnover has resulted in changes in relative abundance and in size class frequencies of certain species.

--- SENTENCE 1322 ---
The non-*Montipora* spp have increased in size and the branching *Pocillopora* spp have become less relatively abundant over time.

--- SENTENCE 1323 ---
Overall, the coral community was very healthy and diverse with characteristic low profile coral forms (massive and encrusting) dominating this high exposure site.

--- SENTENCE 1324 ---

--- SENTENCE 1363 ---
There is evidence that there has been relatively high recruitment of these species, and the shift towards larger sizes is consistent with the relative growth rates of these species.

#### **SUMMARY**

The structure of the coral community at Lepula has not changed substantially from 1995 with the continuation of dominance of massive and encrusting growth forms.

--- SENTENCE 1393 ---
However, species turnover has not remained the same with some significant shifts in relative abundance, as well as changes in size class frequencies between surveys.

--- SENTENCE 1394 ---
The non-*Montipora* spp have increased in size and the branching forms have become more relatively abundant over time.

--- SENTENCE 1395 ---
Overall, the coral community was very healthy and diverse with characteristic low profile coral forms dominating this high wave exposure site.

--- SENTENCE 1396 ---
#### Afuli (Tau)

The number of colonies recorded in 2002 at Afuli (750 cols) was higher than in 1995 (425 cols) despite the sampling effort being lower in 2002 (Table 1).

--- SENTENCE 1397 ---
The density of corals was higher in 2002 (30 cols/m²) compared to 1995 (8.5 cols/m², Table 3).

--- SENTENCE 1398 ---
The

number of small unidentified colonies (those without species names) was relatively higher in 1995 compared to 2002 (Appendix 1).
